- FAQ question titles: "What sound do OU and OW make in this list?"; "Is there a rule for choosing OU or OW?"; "How is this OW different from the OW in 'snow'?"; "What comes after OU/OW in the Grade 2 sequence?"

- `shortAnswer`: This list practices both sounds of the oo vowel team: long oo as in moon, food, and zoo, and short oo as in book, look, and foot.
- `description`: The same two letters, oo, spell two different vowel sounds — the long sound heard in moon and the short sound heard in book.
- `readinessSignals`: "Can spell short and long vowels, then notice that oo does not follow either familiar pattern"; "Can hear the difference between the vowel sound in moon and the vowel sound in book"; "Are ready to learn that one spelling can stand for more than one sound"

#### Soft C and Soft G

- Stable id: `grade-2-soft-c-soft-g`
- Canonical URL: `/grade-2/soft-c-soft-g`
- Grade: Grade 2
- Grade Hub section: Core Spelling
- Content role: `grade-unit`
- Sequence/order position: 5 overall in Grade Hub; 5 within Core Spelling; stored content `order`: 50
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/phonics/grade-2-soft-c-soft-g.md`
- Variant classification: `core-spelling`
- Skill ids: `soft-c-soft-g`
- Prerequisite list ids: none
- Next list ids: none
- Related list ids: `grade-2-common-words-3`
- Word count: 12
- Words, in exact stored order: `city, giant, circus, place, face, race, ice, nice, page, cage, large, change`
- `shortAnswer`: This list practices soft c (as in city and place) and soft g (as in page and large), the letters that change sound before e, i, or y.
- `description`: C usually says /k/ and g usually says its hard sound, but before e, i, or y they change — c says /s/ in city, and g says /j/ in page.
- `readinessSignals`: "Can spell the /k/ sound with c, k, and ck and is ready to learn when c changes to a different sound"; "Can hear that the c in city sounds different from the c in cat, and the g in page sounds different from the g in game"; "Is beginning to notice the letter that follows c or g, not just the letter itself"
- FAQ question titles: "What is the rule for soft c and soft g?"; "How is this different from the c/k/ck lesson from Grade 1?"; "Does this connect to the -dge ending from Grade 1?"; "Why are large and change on this list instead of a separate g-only list?"

## Section 4 — Complete canonical Skill library


### Short Vowels


#### Short A Words
- Stable id: `short-a-words`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/short-a-words`
- Skill family: Short Vowels
- Family order: 1
- Skill order within family: 1
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/phonics/short-a-words.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `cat, pan, mad, sad, ran`
- Word count: 5
- Grade curriculum placements: Kindergarten: `kindergarten-short-a-words`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `kindergarten-short-a-words`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `short-e-words`, `silent-e-long-a`
- `shortAnswer`: Short a words use the quick, open /ă/ vowel sound heard in the middle of cat and hat — different from saying the letter's own name, "ay." Within this page's scope, that sound is the letter a in simple three-letter words like cat, pan, and mad, where each letter stands for one sound in order: consonant, vowel, consonant. Below are grouped word families, teaching guidance, and common mix-ups to watch for.
- `description`: How to recognize, group, and teach the short 'a' sound — the quick vowel in cat, hat, and pan — with word families, what to notice, and common mix-ups, for parents, teachers, and tutors.
- FAQ question titles: "What is the short 'a' sound, and why is it usually taught first?"; "Does every letter 'a' make the short-a sound?"; "Should short-a words be sounded out or memorized?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Family | Words |
|---|---|
| -at | cat, hat, mat, sat |
| -an | can, fan, man, ran |
| -ap | cap, map, nap, tap |
| -ad | bad, dad, mad, sad |
| -ag | bag, rag, tag, wag |
| -am | ham, jam, ram |


_Table 2:_

| Short a | Long a |
|---|---|
| cap | cape |
| tap | tape |
| mad | made |


#### Short E Words
- Stable id: `short-e-words`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/short-e-words`
- Skill family: Short Vowels
- Family order: 1
- Skill order within family: 2
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/phonics/short-e-words.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `bed, hen, pet, leg, wet`
- Word count: 5
- Grade curriculum placements: Kindergarten: `kindergarten-short-e-words`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `kindergarten-short-e-words`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `short-i-words`
- `shortAnswer`: Short e words use the quick, flat /ĕ/ vowel sound heard in the middle of bed and hen — different from saying the letter's own name, "ee." Within this page's scope, that sound is the letter e in simple three-letter words like bed, pet, and leg, where each letter stands for one sound in order: consonant, vowel, consonant. Below are grouped word families, a comparison with the easily confused short i sound, and teaching guidance for helping a learner hear the difference.
- `description`: How to recognize, group, and teach the short 'e' sound — the quick vowel in bed, hen, and leg — including word families, the short e/short i mix-up, and teaching guidance, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Are 'said' and 'says' short e words?"; "Should short e words be sounded out or memorized?"; "Are there other short e word families besides these four?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Family | Words |
|---|---|
| -ed | bed, fed, led, red, wed |
| -en | hen, ten, men, pen, den |
| -et | bet, get, net, pet |
| -eg | leg, beg, peg |


_Table 2:_

| Short e | Short i |
|---|---|
| bed | bid |
| pen | pin |
| ten | tin |
| wet | wit |


#### Short I Words
- Stable id: `short-i-words`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/short-i-words`
- Skill family: Short Vowels
- Family order: 1
- Skill order within family: 3
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/phonics/short-i-words.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `pig, sit, win, fish, hill`
- Word count: 5
- Grade curriculum placements: Kindergarten: `kindergarten-short-i-words`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `kindergarten-short-i-words`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `short-e-words`, `silent-e-long-i`
- `shortAnswer`: Short i words use the quick, thin /ĭ/ vowel sound heard in the middle of pig and sit — different from saying the letter's own name, which sounds like "eye." Within this page's scope, that sound is the letter i in simple words like pig, sit, and win, where each letter usually stands for one sound in order: consonant, vowel, consonant. The same sound also carries into slightly longer, everyday words such as fish and hill, where a two-letter consonant sound like sh or a doubled consonant like ll closes the syllable the same way a single consonant does. Below are grouped word families, that digraph and double-consonant pattern, and a comparison with the easily confused short e sound.
- `description`: How to recognize, group, and teach the short 'i' sound — the quick vowel in pig, sit, and fish — including word families, digraph and double-consonant endings, and the short i/short e mix-up, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "What is the short 'i' sound, and how is it different from the letter's name?"; "Are 'fish' and 'hill' still short-i words even though they have more than three letters?"; "Should short i words be sounded out or memorized?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Family | Words                   |
| ------ | ----------------------- |
| -it    | bit, fit, hit, kit, sit |
| -ig    | big, dig, fig, pig, wig |
| -in    | bin, fin, pin, tin, win |
| -ip    | dip, hip, lip, rip, tip |
| -ish   | dish, fish, wish        |


_Table 2:_

| Short i | Short e |
| ------- | ------- |
| bit     | bet     |
| pin     | pen     |
| tin     | ten     |
| win     | wen     |


#### Short O Words
- Stable id: `short-o-words`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/short-o-words`
- Skill family: Short Vowels
- Family order: 1
- Skill order within family: 4
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/phonics/short-o-words.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `hot, dog, rock, pond, moth`
- Word count: 5
- Grade curriculum placements: Kindergarten: `kindergarten-short-o-words`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `kindergarten-short-o-words`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `short-u-words`, `silent-e-long-o`
- `shortAnswer`: Short o words use the round, open /ŏ/ vowel sound heard in the middle of hot and dog — different from saying the letter's own name, which sounds like "oh." Within this page's scope, that sound is the letter o in simple words like hot, top, and rock, where each letter usually stands for one sound in order: consonant, vowel, consonant, or consonant, vowel, and a short consonant ending like -ck or -nd. Below are grouped word families, a comparison with the easily confused short u sound, and a brief note on how this vowel shifts in some regional accents.
- `description`: How to recognize, group, and teach the short 'o' sound — the round vowel in hot, dog, and rock — including word families, the short o/short u mix-up, and a note on regional pronunciation, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "What is the short 'o' sound, and how is it different from the letter's name?"; "Why do some people pronounce short o closer to the sound in 'saw'?"; "Should short o words be sounded out or memorized?"

| Family | Words                        |
| ------ | ---------------------------- |

| -op    | hop, mop, pop, top           |
| -og    | dog, fog, jog, log           |
| -ock   | dock, lock, rock, sock       |


_Table 2:_

| Short o | Short u |
| ------- | ------- |
| hot     | hut     |
| cop     | cup     |
| rob     | rub     |
| dock    | duck    |


#### Short U Words
- Stable id: `short-u-words`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/short-u-words`
- Skill family: Short Vowels
- Family order: 1
- Skill order within family: 5
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/phonics/short-u-words.md`

- Linked Grade Unit ids: `kindergarten-short-u-words`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `short-o-words`, `silent-e-long-u`
- `shortAnswer`: Short u words use the quick, central /ŭ/ vowel sound heard in the middle of sun and cup — different from saying the letter's own name, which sounds like "you." Within this page's scope, that sound is usually spelled with the letter u in simple words like sun, cup, and drum, though a set of very common words — including son, love, and come — spell this same sound with the letter o instead. Below are grouped word families, that o-spelling exception, and a comparison with the easily confused short o sound.
- `description`: How to recognize, group, and teach the short 'u' sound — the central vowel in sun, cup, and drum — including word families, common words that spell this sound with 'o' instead of 'u', and the short u/short o mix-up, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "What is the short 'u' sound, and how is it different from the letter's name?"; "Why do words like 'son' and 'love' sound like short u but are spelled with 'o'?"; "Should short u words be sounded out or memorized?"

| ------ | ----------------------- |

| -un    | bun, fun, run, sun      |
| -ub    | cub, rub, tub           |
| -ust   | dust, just, must, trust |


_Table 2:_

| Short u | Short o |
| ------- | ------- |
| hut     | hot     |
| cup     | cop     |
| rub     | rob     |
| duck    | dock    |

- `shortAnswer`: A digraph is two letters that work together as one spelling unit for a single sound. This list practices the CH digraph, where C and H together spell one sound — not the /k/ sound C usually spells or the /h/ sound H usually spells — heard at the start of chin and the end of lunch. CH begins with a brief stopping sound, almost like a quiet "t," which sets it apart from smoother digraphs like SH.
- `description`: How to recognize, group, and teach the CH digraph — the single sound at the start of chin and the end of lunch — including how it differs from a consonant blend, a comparison with SH, and common mix-ups, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Doesn't the word 'chef' break this pattern?"; "How can I tell whether two letters are a digraph or a blend?"; "Should CH words be sounded out or memorized?"

- `shortAnswer`: A digraph is two letters that work together as one spelling unit for a single sound. This list practices the SH digraph, where S and H together spell one smooth, continuous sound — not the hissing /s/ sound S usually spells or the breathy /h/ sound H usually spells — heard at the start of ship and the end of wish. Unlike CH, which starts with a brief stop, SH flows evenly from start to finish.
- `description`: How to recognize, group, and teach the SH digraph — the smooth, continuous sound at the start of ship and the end of wish — including a comparison with CH and common mix-ups, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Does every SH sound get spelled with the letters S and H?"; "How can I tell whether S and H are a digraph here, or just two letters that happen to sit next to each other?"; "Should SH words be sounded out or memorized?"

|---|---|

#### TH Digraph Words
- Stable id: `digraph-th-words`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/th-digraph-words`
- Skill family: Consonant Digraphs
- Family order: 2
- Skill order within family: 3
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/phonics/digraph-th-words.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `that, this, them, think, math, bath`
- Word count: 6
- Grade curriculum placements: Kindergarten: `kindergarten-consonant-digraphs`; Grade 1: `grade-1-consonant-digraphs-final-ck`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `kindergarten-consonant-digraphs`, `grade-1-consonant-digraphs-final-ck`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `digraph-sh-words`, `digraph-ch-words`, `digraph-wh-words`
- `shortAnswer`: A digraph is two letters that work together as one spelling unit for a single sound — but TH is unusual because it stands for two closely related sounds, spelled exactly the same way. One version is voiced, where the vocal cords buzz, heard in that, this, and them. The other is unvoiced, a quieter version with no buzz, heard in think, math, and bath. Both are written TH either way, so a learner does not need to identify which version a word uses in order to spell it correctly.
- `description`: How to recognize, group, and teach the TH digraph — one spelling for two related sounds, the voiced sound in that and they and the unvoiced sound in think and bath — with guidance on teaching both without overwhelming a learner, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Does a child need to know whether a TH is voiced or unvoiced to spell it correctly?"; "Is there an easy way to feel the difference between the two TH sounds?"; "My child says 'wif' for 'with' — should I correct that before practicing TH spelling?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Voiced TH (throat buzzes) | Unvoiced TH (no buzz) |
|---|---|
| that, this, them | think, math, bath |


_Table 2:_

| Position | Example words |
|---|---|
| Beginning | that, this, think |
| End | math, bath, path |


#### WH Digraph Words
- Stable id: `digraph-wh-words`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/wh-digraph-words`
- Skill family: Consonant Digraphs
- Family order: 2
- Skill order within family: 4

- Word count: 6
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 1: `grade-1-consonant-digraphs-final-ck`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-1-consonant-digraphs-final-ck`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `digraph-sh-words`, `digraph-ch-words`, `digraph-th-words`
- `shortAnswer`: A digraph is two letters that work together as one spelling unit for a single sound. This list practices the WH digraph, where W and H together spell a sound that, for most speakers today, matches a plain /w/, as in what and when. In a small group of words — including who, whom, whose, and whole — the pattern works differently: the W is silent, and the word begins with an /h/ sound instead. WH is most useful to learn as a bounded set of everyday words, especially question words, rather than as a rule that applies to any new word with a /w/ sound.
- `description`: How to recognize, group, and teach the WH digraph — the sound at the start of most question words — including the small group of words where the W is silent, such as who, whom, whose, and whole, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Why does 'who' sound completely different from 'what' or 'when'?"; "Is WH supposed to sound different from a plain W, like in 'wag' or 'went'?"; "Should WH words be sounded out or memorized?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| WH spelling the /w/ sound | WH spelling the /h/ sound |
|---|---|
| what, when, where, whip, while | who, whom, whose, whole |


### Consonant Blends


#### Beginning Blends
- Stable id: `beginning-blends`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/beginning-blends`
- Skill family: Consonant Blends
- Family order: 3
- Skill order within family: 1
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/phonics/beginning-blends.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `clap, plum, grab, crab, stop, swim`
- Word count: 6
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 1: `grade-1-beginning-consonant-blends`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-1-beginning-consonant-blends`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `ending-blends`, `digraph-sh-words`, `digraph-ch-words`
- `shortAnswer`: A beginning consonant blend is two (sometimes three) consonant sounds that sit right next to each other at the start of a word, with each sound still heard — unlike a digraph, where two letters fuse into a single new sound. In clap, both the /k/ and the /l/ are heard before the vowel; in stop, both /s/ and /t/ are heard. This Skill practices common beginning blends such as cl, gr, and st using a small set of familiar words, including clap, grab, and stop.
- `description`: How to recognize, group, and teach beginning consonant blends — like cl, gr, and st — including how a blend differs from a beginning digraph, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Are three-letter blends like scr and str part of this Skill?"; "Why doesn't a beginning blend turn into one new sound the way a digraph does?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Blend group | Blends | Example words |
|---|---|---|
| L-blends | bl, cl, fl, gl, pl, sl | clap, flag, plum |
| R-blends | br, cr, dr, fr, gr, pr, tr | grab, crab, drum |
| S-blends | sm, sn, sp, st, sw | stop, swim, spin |


_Table 2:_

| Beginning blend | Beginning digraph |
|---|---|
| stop | shop |
| trip | chip |


#### Ending Blends
- Stable id: `ending-blends`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/ending-blends`
- Skill family: Consonant Blends
- Family order: 3
- Skill order within family: 2
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/phonics/ending-blends.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `hand, jump, milk, belt, desk, gift`
- Word count: 6
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 1: `grade-1-ending-consonant-blends`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-1-ending-consonant-blends`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `beginning-blends`, `digraph-ch-words`
- `shortAnswer`: An ending consonant blend is two consonant sounds that sit right next to each other at the end of a word, with each sound still heard — as in the /n/ and /d/ in hand, or the /m/ and /p/ in jump. Unlike a beginning blend, an ending blend comes after the vowel, is spoken last, and is easy to trail off or clip in casual speech, which makes it worth listening for carefully. This Skill practices common ending blends such as nd, mp, and sk using a small set of familiar words, including hand, jump, and desk.
- `description`: How to recognize, group, and teach ending consonant blends — like nd, mp, and sk — including why they're often harder to hear than beginning blends, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Why do ending blends get left off more than beginning blends?"; "Is the nk in words like sink and pink really two separate consonant sounds?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Blend group | Blends | Example words |
|---|---|---|
| Nasal blends (often clipped in fast speech) | mp, nd, nk, nt | jump, hand, sink, tent |
| Other ending blends | ft, lk, lt, sk | gift, milk, belt, desk |


### Common Spelling Patterns


#### CK, Double Letters, and TCH/DGE Word Endings
- Stable id: `ck-tch-dge-word-endings`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/ck-tch-dge-word-endings`
- Skill family: Common Spelling Patterns
- Family order: 4
- Skill order within family: 1
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/phonics/ck-tch-dge-word-endings.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `back, duck, bell, moss, cliff, buzz, catch, pitch, badge, bridge`
- Word count: 10
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 1: `grade-1-floss-rule`; Grade 1: `grade-1-consonant-digraphs-final-ck`; Grade 1: `grade-1-tch-dge-ending-rules`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-1-floss-rule`, `grade-1-consonant-digraphs-final-ck`, `grade-1-tch-dge-ending-rules`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `digraph-ch-words`
- `shortAnswer`: This Skill covers four separate spelling choices that all show up at the end of a one-syllable word, right after a short vowel: ck for the /k/ sound (back, duck), doubling the final letter for /f/, /l/, /s/, or sometimes /z/ (bell, moss, cliff, buzz), tch for the /ch/ sound (catch, pitch), and dge for the /j/ sound (badge, bridge). They are four different spelling decisions, not one rule — each answers the same question (what goes right after a short vowel at the end of a short word) in its own way, and each has a few familiar exceptions, like which and if.
- `description`: Four related one-syllable ending spellings, right after a short vowel: ck for /k/ (back, duck), doubled f/l/s/z (bell, moss, cliff, buzz), tch for /ch/ (catch, pitch), and dge for /j/ (badge, bridge).
- FAQ question titles: "Are ck and k interchangeable at the end of a word?"; "Why don't common words like if, yes, and bus double their final letter?"; "How is ch different from tch at the end of a word?"; "How is ge different from dge at the end of a word?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Condition | Spelling | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| /k/ at the end of a one-syllable word, right after a single short vowel | ck | back, duck, rock, pick |
| /k/ at the end of a one-syllable word, right after another consonant | k | milk, pink, desk, bank |
| /k/ at the end of a one-syllable word, right after a long vowel or vowel team | k | bike, leak, book, cake |


_Table 2:_

| Condition | Spelling | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| /f/, /l/, or /s/ at the end of a one-syllable word, right after a single short vowel | double the letter | staff, bell, moss, cliff |
| /z/ at the end of a one-syllable word, right after a single short vowel | sometimes doubled | buzz, fuzz, jazz |
| Any of these sounds after a consonant blend, not directly after the vowel | single letter | shelf, golf, else |


_Table 3:_

| Condition | Spelling | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| /ch/ at the end of a one-syllable word, right after a single short vowel | tch | catch, pitch, notch, hutch |
| /ch/ at the end of a one-syllable word, right after a consonant | ch | bench, lunch, ranch |
| /ch/ at the end of a one-syllable word, right after a long vowel or vowel team | ch | reach, coach, pooch |


_Table 4:_

| Condition | Spelling | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| /j/ at the end of a one-syllable word, right after a single short vowel | dge | badge, bridge, lodge, fudge |
| /j/ at the end of a one-syllable word, right after a consonant such as n or l | ge | change, bulge, hinge |
| /j/ at the end of a one-syllable word, right after a long vowel | ge | page, cage, huge |


#### Silent Letters
- Stable id: `silent-letters`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/silent-letters`
- Skill family: Common Spelling Patterns

- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: none
- `shortAnswer`: In words like write, know, and thumb, one letter is written but not said out loud: the w in wr, the k in kn, and the b after m at the end of a word. These are called silent letters. Spelling them from sound alone is not enough — the pattern has to be remembered as part of the word, because leaving the letter out is often a real, different word, like rap instead of wrap or not instead of knot.
- `description`: How the wr, kn, and mb spelling patterns work — a letter is written but not pronounced — including why dropping the letter often spells a different real word, common mixed-up spellings, and how this differs from silent e.
- FAQ question titles: "Why do English words keep letters that nobody says out loud?"; "Is a silent letter the same thing as the silent h in a digraph like sh or th?"; "Should a young child's early, invented spelling include the silent letter, or is it fine if they leave it out at first?"; "Besides wr, kn, and mb, what other silent-letter patterns will children run into later?"

|---|---|---|

| write | rite | w |


_Table 2:_

|---|---|---|

#### Soft C and Soft G
- Stable id: `soft-c-soft-g`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/soft-c-soft-g`
- Skill family: Common Spelling Patterns
- Family order: 4
- Skill order within family: 3
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/phonics/soft-c-soft-g.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `city, ice, icy, giant, page, gym`
- Word count: 6
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 2: `grade-2-soft-c-soft-g`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-2-soft-c-soft-g`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `ck-tch-dge-word-endings`, `silent-e-long-a`, `silent-e-long-i`
- `shortAnswer`: Soft c and soft g are a real, teachable spelling pattern: when the letter right after c or g is e, i, or y, that c or g usually changes from its hard sound to a softer one. C changes from /k/ (as in cat) to /s/ (as in city). G changes from /g/ (as in game) to /j/ (as in page). The pattern is very reliable for c — soft c almost always shows up before e, i, or y. G is less reliable: common words like get, give, girl, and gift keep the hard /g/ sound even though g is followed by e or i.
- `description`: Why city sounds different from cat, and page sounds different from game: c and g usually change from their hard sounds to soft ones when the next letter is e, i, or y.
- FAQ question titles: "Is this the same as the c/k/ck spelling choice taught earlier?"; "Does the pattern still hold when an ending like -ing or -ed is added?"; "Do any other consonants change sound based on the letter that follows, the way c and g do?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| The next letter is... | *c* usually says | *g* usually says |
|---|---|---|
| e, i, or y | /s/ — soft (city, ice, icy) | /j/ — soft (giant, page, gym) |
| a, o, u, or a consonant | /k/ — hard (cat, cot, cup) | /g/ — hard (game, got, gum) |


### Silent E


#### Long A Silent E Words
- Stable id: `silent-e-long-a`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/long-a-silent-e`
- Skill family: Silent E
- Family order: 5
- Skill order within family: 1
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/phonics/silent-e-long-a.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `cake, gate, name, plate, shape`
- Word count: 5
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 1: `grade-1-long-vowels-silent-e`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-1-long-vowels-silent-e`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: `short-a-words`
- Related Skill ids: `short-a-words`, `soft-c-soft-g`
- `shortAnswer`: A silent e sits at the end of a word without making its own sound, but it changes the vowel before it. In words like cake, gate, and plate, that final silent e signals that the earlier vowel says its own name — long a — instead of the short a sound in cat. This pattern is called silent e or vowel-consonant-e (VCe); both names describe the same spelling shape.
- `description`: How the silent-e pattern turns a short vowel into a long 'a' sound, as in cake and gate — with word families, the short-a-to-long-a contrast, common mistakes, and a teaching routine, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Does the silent e in cake do anything if it isn't pronounced?"; "Is silent e the only way to spell the long a sound?"; "Why does have keep its silent e but still sound like short a?"

|---|---|

| -ate | gate, late, date, plate, skate |
| -ame | flame, game, name, same, tame |
| -ape | cape, grape, shape, tape |
| -ave | brave, cave, gave, save, wave |


_Table 2:_

|---|---|
| cap | cape |
| tap | tape |
| plan | plane |
| past | paste |

- `shortAnswer`: A silent e at the end of a word makes no sound of its own, but it changes the vowel earlier in the word. In words like bike, time, and hive, that final silent e signals that the i says its own name — long i — instead of the short i sound in sit. This is the same silent-e, or vowel-consonant-e (VCe), pattern taught with long a, applied to a different vowel.
- `description`: How the silent-e pattern that produces long a also applies to long i, as in bike and time — including why learners often over-apply the rule, word families, exceptions like give and live, and a teaching routine, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Why do children sometimes add a silent e to words that don't need one?"; "Why do give and live keep a silent e but still have a short i sound?"; "Is silent e the only way to spell the long i sound?"

- `shortAnswer`: A silent e at the end of a word makes no sound of its own, but it changes the vowel earlier in the word. In words like home, hope, and note, that final silent e signals that the o says its own name — long o — instead of a short sound. A handful of very common words, including gone, done, and come, look like they should follow this pattern but don't.
- `description`: How the silent-e pattern produces the long o sound in common words like home, hope, and stone — including well-known exceptions like gone and come, word families, and a teaching routine, for parents and teachers.

- `shortAnswer`: A silent e at the end of a word makes no sound of its own, but it changes the vowel earlier in the word. Long u silent-e words make one of two sounds: /yoo/, as in cube and cute, or plain /oo/, as in rule and flute — both count as the same silent-e spelling pattern, even though the vowel sound itself differs by word.
- `description`: How the silent-e pattern for long u produces two different vowel sounds — /yoo/ in cube and cute, /oo/ in rule and flute — including which letters tend to predict each one, dialect variation, and a teaching routine, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Why do rule and flute sound different from cube and mule, even though they all use the same silent-e pattern?"; "Does everyone pronounce these words the same way?"; "Is silent e the only way to spell the long u sound?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Says "yoo" | Says "oo" |
|---|---|
| cube | rule |
| cute | flute |
| huge | prune |
| mule | |
| fuse | |


### Vowel Teams


#### AI and AY Words
- Stable id: `vowel-teams-ai-ay`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/ai-ay-vowel-teams`
- Skill family: Vowel Teams

- `shortAnswer`: AI and AY are two-letter vowel teams that spell the long a sound — the sound that says its own name, as in rain, mail, play, and stay. In common one-syllable words, ai is typically found in the middle of the word or syllable, while ay is typically found at the end. Neither letter pair makes its own separate sound; together, the two letters spell one long vowel sound.
- `description`: How the AI and AY vowel teams spell the long 'a' sound — including the reliable word-position tendency behind the two spellings, how it applies once a word grows longer, and the contrast with long A silent E, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Why does 'wait' use ai and not ay if the sound comes before a consonant?"; "Does the AI/AY position tendency still apply to longer, multisyllable words?"

| Spelling | Where it appears | Words |
|---|---|---|
| ai | Inside the word or syllable | chain, mail, nail, paint, rain, snail, train, wait |
| ay | At the end of the word | day, play, stay, way |

- `shortAnswer`: EE and EA are two-letter vowel teams that both commonly spell the long e sound — the sound that says its own name, as in tree, feel, beach, and clean. Unlike some other vowel teams, there is no dependable rule for knowing which of the two spellings a particular word uses; recognizing and practicing each word is the most reliable strategy.
- `description`: Why EE and EA both commonly spell the long 'e' sound with no reliable rule for choosing between them, and how recognition and word-family practice — not a position rule — build accurate spelling, for parents and teachers.

It does not cover the different, unrelated sound ow also spells in words like cow and how — that gliding sound belongs to a separate pattern, OU and OW Words.
- `description`: How OA and long-O OW spell the long 'o' sound — and how to tell that OW apart from the unrelated sound OW makes in words like cow — with word groupings, a teaching routine, and the position tendency behind the two spellings, for parents and teachers.

- `shortAnswer`: OI and OY are two-letter vowel teams that spell the same gliding sound — the sound heard in oil, coin, boy, and enjoy. OY typically ends a syllable, whether that's the end of a short word like boy or the end of the first syllable in a longer word like royal; OI is typically used everywhere else.
- `description`: How OI and OY spell the same gliding vowel sound heard in oil, coin, boy, and enjoy — and the syllable-position tendency behind the two spellings, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Is the rule really 'OI in the middle, OY at the end'?"; "How is OI/OY different from OA/OW?"; "Why does 'royal' use OY instead of OI if the sound isn't at the very end of the word?"

| oy | At the end of a syllable | boy, joy, enjoy, royal, loyal |

#### IE and IGH Words
- Stable id: `ie-and-igh-words`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/ie-and-igh-words`
- Skill family: Vowel Teams

- Word count: 8
- Grade curriculum placements: none
- Linked Grade Unit ids: none
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `silent-e-long-i`
- `shortAnswer`: IE and IGH can both spell long i. IE often ends a short base word, as in pie and tie, while IGH commonly appears within a word or at the end of a syllable, as in night and high. These are useful tendencies, not rules that identify every long-i spelling.
- `description`: Learn when ie and igh spell long i, compare them with silent e and final y, and avoid common long-i spelling mix-ups.
- FAQ question titles: "Does IE always spell long I?"; "Why does cried end in IED if cry ends in Y?"; "Do the G and H make separate sounds in night?"

_Table 1:_

| Spelling | Useful tendency | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **ie** | Often ends a short base word | pie, tie, lie, die |
| **igh** | Commonly appears within a word or at the end of a syllable | high, sigh, light, night, right, might, sight, bright, flight |


_Table 2:_

| Word shape | Examples | What carries long I? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Silent e | time, ride | A final silent e signals the earlier vowel; see [Long I Silent E Words](/skills/long-i-silent-e). |
| Final **ie** | pie, tie | The final letter pair spells long i. |
| **igh** | high, night | The three-letter spelling represents long i. |
| Final **y** | my, fly | Final y represents long i in these short words. |


#### OO Words
- Stable id: `oo-words`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/oo-words`

- Related Skill ids: `ou-and-ow-words`
- `shortAnswer`: The letters oo most commonly spell two different vowel sounds, and neither one is the 'main' or default sound: a longer sound, as in moon and food, and a shorter sound, as in book and foot. There's no spelling clue on the page that tells a child which sound a new oo word uses — the word has to be recognized or tried both ways and checked against a word the learner already knows. A small number of oo words use neither sound and aren't covered here.
- `description`: How the same two letters, oo, spell two different vowel sounds — the sound heard in moon and food, and the different sound heard in book and foot — with no visual clue that tells them apart, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Which oo sound should a child learn first — is one of them the 'normal' one?"; "Is there any way to predict which sound a new oo word uses?"; "Why do some people say 'roof' or 'root' with the long sound and others with the short sound?"; "Are there oo words that don't use either sound taught here?"

#### AU and AW Words
- Stable id: `au-and-aw-words`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/au-and-aw-words`

- Prerequisite Skill ids: `oo-words`
- Related Skill ids: `oo-words`
- `shortAnswer`: AU and AW spell the same vowel sound — the sound heard in saw, draw, hawk, and cause. In the common words taught here, aw is typically found at the end of a word or right before n or l, while au is typically found in the middle. It's a genuinely useful tendency, not an absolute rule — a few common words, like haul, break it.
- `description`: How AU and AW spell the same vowel sound heard in saw and cause — and the position tendency, and its genuine exceptions, behind the two spellings, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "How reliable is the aw-before-n-or-l tendency, really?"; "Are augh words like caught and taught included here?"; "Doesn't 'laugh' break the rule that au spells the saw/cause sound?"; "Why is 'author' included if au isn't at the very start or end of the word?"

_Table 1:_

| Spelling | Where it typically appears | Words |
|---|---|---|
| aw | End of a word, or before n or l | saw, draw, lawn, crawl, hawk |
| au | Middle of a word | cause, author |


### R-Controlled Vowels


#### R-Controlled AR Words
- Stable id: `r-controlled-ar`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/r-controlled-ar-words`
- Skill family: R-Controlled Vowels

- Skill order within family: 1

- Prerequisite Skill ids: `short-a-words`
- Related Skill ids: `r-controlled-or`
- `shortAnswer`: AR words use the r-controlled vowel sound heard in car and farm — a sound the letter r creates by changing the vowel just before it, different from the short a sound in cat. Within this page's scope, that's the letter combination a-r in common one-syllable words like car, farm, and yard, where ar reliably makes this one sound almost every time it appears. Below are grouped example words, a comparison with short a, the one common exception worth knowing (words like war and warm), and teaching guidance.
- `description`: How to teach the AR r-controlled vowel pattern — one of the most reliable r-controlled spellings, as in car, farm, and yard — including the sound, word families, a comparison with short a, and the one common exception (war, warm) worth knowing, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Should AR words be sounded out or memorized?"; "Why don't war and warm rhyme with car and farm?"; "What about words like care and share — are those AR words too?"

| -ard | hard, yard |
| -ark | dark, park, shark |
| -arm | arm, farm |
| -art | art, cart, start |


_Table 2:_

| Short a | R-controlled AR |
|---|---|
| cat | car |
| hat | hard |

- `shortAnswer`: OR words use the r-controlled vowel sound heard in corn and short — a sound the letter r creates by changing the vowel just before it, different from the short o sound in cot. Within this page's scope, that's the letter combination o-r in common words like corn, north, and story, where or reliably makes this one sound in most words. A well-known exception is the small group of words spelled with wor-, like word and work, which make a different sound instead. Below are grouped example words, a comparison with short o, that exception, and teaching guidance.
- `description`: How to teach the OR r-controlled vowel pattern, as in corn, short, and morning — including the sound, word families, a comparison with short o, the word/work exception, and a note on genuine regional pronunciation variation, for parents and teachers.

#### R-Controlled ER, IR, and UR Words
- Stable id: `r-controlled-er-ir-ur`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/r-controlled-er-ir-ur-words`
- Skill family: R-Controlled Vowels
- Family order: 7

- Content role: `skill`

- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-2-two-syllable-words`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `r-controlled-ar`, `r-controlled-or`
- `shortAnswer`: ER, IR, and UR are three different spellings for one r-controlled vowel sound — the sound heard in her, bird, and turn (in mainstream American English, where the r is always pronounced). There is no dependable rule for predicting which of the three spellings a given word uses; the practical strategy is recognizing and remembering each word through repeated reading and writing, not applying a sound-based test. Below are the three spellings grouped separately, a note on how the sound differs slightly in stressed words versus longer words, and teaching guidance.
- `description`: Three spellings, one sound — the ER pattern in her, the IR pattern in bird, and the UR pattern in turn all make the same r-controlled vowel sound, with no reliable rule for choosing between them, for parents and teachers.
- FAQ question titles: "Is there a rule for choosing ER, IR, or UR?"; "Why are ER, IR, and UR taught together instead of as three separate lists?"; "Do word, work, and world belong on this page even though they're spelled with or?"

### Word Building and Endings


#### Plural Words with -s and -es
- Stable id: `plurals`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/plurals`
- Skill family: Word Building and Endings
- Family order: 9
- Skill order within family: 1
- Content role: `skill`

- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `cats, dogs, books, buses, boxes, dishes, lunches`
- Word count: 7

- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-1-inflectional-endings-s-es`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: none
- `shortAnswer`: Regular plural nouns name more than one. Most add -s to the singular word, as in cat → cats. Nouns ending in hissing or buzzing sounds commonly spelled s, x, z, sh, or ch usually add -es, as in box → boxes and dish → dishes.
- `description`: Learn how regular nouns form plurals with -s or -es, why some endings need an extra syllable, and which familiar plurals fall outside this pattern.
- FAQ question titles: "Does every plural end in -s or -es?"; "Why does bus become buses but book becomes books?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Singular noun | Change | Plural noun |
|---|---|---|
| *cat* | add *-s* | *cats* |
| *dog* | add *-s* | *dogs* |
| *book* | add *-s* | *books* |
| *box* | add *-es* | *boxes* |
| *dish* | add *-es* | *dishes* |


_Table 2:_

| Add *-s* | Add *-es* |
|---|---|
| *book → books* | *box → boxes* |
| *cup → cups* | *bus → buses* |
| *tree → trees* | *brush → brushes* |


#### Words Ending in -ed and -ing

- `shortAnswer`: The endings -ed and -ing attach to an action word. -Ed commonly shows an action in the past, as in jump → jumped. -Ing helps show an action in progress, as in jump → jumping. This Skill begins with words whose base spelling stays unchanged.
- `description`: Learn how -ed and -ing attach to action-word bases, what the endings show, and how to preserve an unchanged base word in clear beginning examples.

- Skill order within family: 3
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/grade-level/common-suffixes.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `helpful, careless, kindness, enjoyment, faster, fastest`
- Word count: 6
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 3: `grade-3-suffix-words`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-3-suffix-words`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `suffix-spelling-changes`
- `shortAnswer`: A suffix is a meaningful ending added to a base word. Derivational suffixes can build a word with a new meaning or function: help + ful = helpful and kind + ness = kindness. Comparative -er and -est work differently by inflecting a describing word for comparison: fast → faster → fastest.
- `description`: Learn how common suffixes build words: -ful and -less add meaning, -ness and -ment often form nouns, and comparative -er and -est inflect describing words.
- FAQ question titles: "Does -ful have one l or two?"; "Are all word endings suffixes?"; "How is this different from Spelling Rules for Adding Suffixes?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Suffix | Useful meaning or job | Word equation | Whole-word meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| *-ful* | full of, having, or showing | *help + ful = helpful* | giving help |
| *-less* | without or lacking | *care + less = careless* | without enough care |
| *-ness* | forms a noun for a state or quality | *kind + ness = kindness* | the quality of being kind |
| *-ment* | often forms a noun for an action, process, or result | *enjoy + ment = enjoyment* | the state or result of enjoying |


_Table 2:_

| Base describing word | Compare two | Compare within a larger group |
|---|---|---|
| *fast* | *faster* | *fastest* |
| *slow* | *slower* | *slowest* |
| *small* | *smaller* | *smallest* |


#### Spelling Rules for Adding Suffixes
- Stable id: `suffix-spelling-changes`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/suffix-spelling-changes`
- Skill family: Word Building and Endings
- Family order: 9
- Skill order within family: 4
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/grade-level/suffix-spelling-changes.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `running, stopped, making, hopeful, carried, carrying`
- Word count: 6
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 3: `grade-3-dropping-silent-e`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-3-dropping-silent-e`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `ed-and-ing`, `common-suffixes`
- `shortAnswer`: Some base words change spelling before a suffix is added. A short-vowel one-syllable word ending in one consonant often doubles it before a vowel suffix (run → running); a final silent e is often dropped before a vowel suffix (make → making); and consonant-plus-y usually changes to i before many suffixes (carry → carried), but not before -ing.
- `description`: Learn how to test a base word before adding a suffix: when to double a final consonant, drop final silent e, or change consonant-plus-y to i.
- FAQ question titles: "Do these rules apply every time a suffix is added?"; "Why does play become played instead of plaied?"; "Why is carrying spelled with y?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Base word | Check | Result |
|---|---|---|
| *run* | short vowel + one final consonant + *-ing* | *running* |
| *stop* | short vowel + one final consonant + *-ed* | *stopped* |
| *big* | short vowel + one final consonant + *-er* | *bigger* |


_Table 2:_

| Base word | Vowel suffix | Result |
|---|---|---|
| *make* | *-ing* | *making* |
| *hope* | *-ed* | *hoped* |
| *safe* | *-er* | *safer* |


_Table 3:_

| Base word | Check | Result |
|---|---|---|
| *carry* | consonant + *y*, then *-ed* | *carried* |
| *happy* | consonant + *y*, then *-ness* | *happiness* |
| *city* | consonant + *y*, then plural *-es* | *cities* |


#### Compound Words
- Stable id: `compound-words`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/compound-words`
- Skill family: Word Building and Endings
- Family order: 9
- Skill order within family: 5
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/grade-level/compound-words.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `sunshine, backpack, notebook, snowman, sunset, toothbrush`
- Word count: 6
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 2: `grade-2-list-02`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-2-list-02`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: none
- `shortAnswer`: A compound word combines two words into one larger word with a connected meaning: sun + shine = sunshine and note + book = notebook. Recognizing both meaningful parts gives a learner a reliable way to spell many longer words.
- `description`: Learn how two meaningful words build one compound, how the parts support spelling and meaning, and why a compound is more than an ordinary syllable split.
- FAQ question titles: "Are all compound words written as one word?"; "Is every two-syllable word a compound word?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| First word | Second word | Compound | Meaning connection |
|---|---|---|---|
| *sun* | *shine* | *sunshine* | light that comes from the sun |
| *note* | *book* | *notebook* | a book for notes |
| *snow* | *man* | *snowman* | a figure made from snow |
| *tooth* | *brush* | *toothbrush* | a brush used for teeth |


_Table 2:_

| Genuine compound | Not a compound |
|---|---|
| *rain + bow = rainbow* | *robot* has syllables, not two meaningful word parts |
| *week + end = weekend* | *winter* cannot be explained as two component words |
| *bed + room = bedroom* | *basket* is not *bas + ket* |


#### Contractions
- Stable id: `contractions`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/contractions`
- Skill family: Word Building and Endings
- Family order: 9
- Skill order within family: 6
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/grade-level/contractions.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `can't, don't, didn't, I'm, I'll, she's, won't`
- Word count: 7
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 2: `grade-2-contractions`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-2-contractions`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: none
- `shortAnswer`: A contraction is a shortened form of two words. An apostrophe marks where one or more letters have been omitted: do not → don't, I am → I'm, and she will → she'll. Expanding the contraction helps a learner place the apostrophe.
- `description`: Learn how two words contract into a shorter form, how an apostrophe marks omitted letters, and how to spell common regular patterns and won't.
- FAQ question titles: "Why does won't not look like will not?"; "Does every apostrophe make a contraction?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Two words | What is omitted | Contraction |
|---|---|---|
| *do not* | the *o* in *not* | *don't* |
| *did not* | the *o* in *not* | *didn't* |
| *I am* | the *a* in *am* | *I'm* |
| *she is* | the *i* in *is* | *she's* |
| *I will* | *w-i* in *will* | *I'll* |


### Prefixes


#### Un- and Re- Prefixes
- Stable id: `un-and-re-prefixes`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/un-and-re-prefixes`
- Skill family: Prefixes
- Family order: 10
- Skill order within family: 1
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/grade-level/un-and-re-prefixes.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `unfair, unhappy, unlock, redo, reread, retell`
- Word count: 6
- Grade curriculum placements: none
- Linked Grade Unit ids: none
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `common-suffixes`, `greek-and-latin-roots`
- `shortAnswer`: A prefix is a word part added to the front of a base word to build a new, related word. Un- usually means "not" (unhappy) or "reverse" (unlock), and re- usually means "again" (reread, retell). Unlike many suffixes, un- and re- attach to the base word without changing its spelling.
- `description`: What a prefix is and how the un- and re- prefixes change meaning — plus how a prefix differs from a suffix, a syllable, and a root.
- FAQ question titles: "Are im- and in- the same prefix as un-?"; "Does every word that starts with un- or re- contain the prefix?"; "Do un- and re- keep appearing beyond this page?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Prefix | Meaning | Word equation | Whole-word meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| *un-* | not / the opposite of | *un- + happy = unhappy* | not happy |
| *un-* | to reverse an action | *un- + lock = unlock* | to undo locking |
| *re-* | again | *re- + read = reread* | to read again |


#### Common Prefixes
- Stable id: `common-prefixes`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/common-prefixes`
- Skill family: Prefixes
- Family order: 10
- Skill order within family: 2
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/grade-level/common-prefixes.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `disagree, predict, misread, interact, submarine, superstar, transport, antifreeze`
- Word count: 8
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 3: `grade-3-prefix-words`; Grade 4: `grade-4-advanced-prefixes`; Grade 5: `grade-5-prefix-suffix-words`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-3-prefix-words`, `grade-4-advanced-prefixes`, `grade-5-prefix-suffix-words`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: `un-and-re-prefixes`
- Related Skill ids: `greek-and-latin-roots`
- `shortAnswer`: This Skill practices common prefixes beyond un- and re-: pre- ("before"), dis- ("not"/"opposite"), mis- ("wrongly"), and the upper-elementary prefixes inter- ("between"), sub- ("under"), super- ("above"), trans- ("across"), and anti- ("against"). Some, like dis- and super-, attach to a whole base word; others, like pre- and trans- in this set, attach to a Latin root instead.
- `description`: Prefixes beyond un- and re- — pre-, dis-, mis-, inter-, sub-, super-, trans-, and anti- — including which ones attach to a whole base word and which attach to a Latin root instead.
- FAQ question titles: "Does this Skill cover every English prefix, such as non- or over-?"; "Is antifreeze written as one word?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Prefix | Meaning | Word equation | Whole-word meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| *dis-* | not / the opposite of | *dis- + agree = disagree* | not in agreement |
| *mis-* | wrongly / badly | *mis- + read = misread* | to read incorrectly |
| *inter-* | between / among | *inter- + act = interact* | to act between or among others |
| *super-* | above / beyond the ordinary | *super- + star = superstar* | a performer whose skill goes beyond the ordinary |
| *anti-* | against | *anti- + freeze = antifreeze* | a liquid that works against freezing |


### Greek and Latin Roots


#### Greek and Latin Roots
- Stable id: `greek-and-latin-roots`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/greek-and-latin-roots`
- Skill family: Greek and Latin Roots
- Family order: 11
- Skill order within family: 1
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/grade-level/greek-and-latin-roots.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `transport, predict, inspect, telephone, telescope, microscope, biography, national, critical`
- Word count: 9
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 3: `grade-3-root-word-families`; Grade 4: `tier-1-roots-and-patterns`; Grade 5: `grade-5-greek-latin-word-parts`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-3-root-word-families`, `tier-1-roots-and-patterns`, `grade-5-greek-latin-word-parts`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `common-prefixes`
- `shortAnswer`: A root carries a word's central meaning. A base is the word part a prefix or suffix attaches to — some bases are complete English words, like help or read, but some Greek and Latin roots are bound and don't normally stand alone, such as dict ("say") in predict. A few roots, like port in transport, also happen to share their spelling with a separate standalone word (port, the harbor) — so a root's meaning comes from the specific word it's inside, not from whether the letters can stand alone elsewhere. Recognizing a root helps with both meaning and spelling, since the same root reappears across related words.
- `description`: How Greek and Latin roots carry a word's core meaning across related words — Latin roots such as port, dict, and spect, Greek roots such as tele, bio, graph, and scope, and derived-word families such as national and critical, where a base word's spelling stays stable even as pronunciation shifts.
- FAQ question titles: "Can the same letters be a root in one word and a standalone word elsewhere?"; "If I know a root's meaning, can I always figure out the whole word's meaning?"; "Why do national and critical belong on a roots page instead of Common Suffixes?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Root | Meaning | Word equation | Whole-word meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| *port* | carry | *trans- + port = transport* | to carry across or from one place to another |
| *dict* | say | *pre- + dict = predict* | to say what will happen before it happens |
| *spect* | look | *in- + spect = inspect* | to look at closely |


_Table 2:_

| Root | Meaning | Word equation | Whole-word meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| *tele* | far | *tele + phone = telephone* | a device for carrying sound across a distance |
| *tele* + *scope* | far + see | *tele + scope = telescope* | a tool for seeing far-off things |
| *scope* | see, watch | *micro- + scope = microscope* | a tool for seeing very small things |
| *bio* + *graph* | life + write | *bio + graph + y = biography* | writing about a person's life |


_Table 3:_

| Family | Origin | What stays stable |
|---|---|---|
| *nation* → *national* | Latin | The spelling *nat-* stays the same, even though the stressed syllable and vowel sound change between *NA-tion* and *NA-tion-al*. |
| *critic* → *critical* | Greek | The spelling *critic-* stays the same, even though *critical* adds a syllable and shifts the word's rhythm. |


### Homophones and Commonly Confused Words


#### Homophones
- Stable id: `homophones`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/homophones`
- Skill family: Homophones and Commonly Confused Words
- Family order: 12
- Skill order within family: 1
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/grade-level/homophones.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `to, too, two, there, their, they're`
- Word count: 6
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 3: `grade-3-homophones`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-3-homophones`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `commonly-confused-words`
- `shortAnswer`: Homophones are words that sound exactly the same but have different spellings and different meanings, such as to, too, and two, or there, their, and they're. Because the sound gives no clue at all, the right spelling has to be chosen from what the word means in the sentence, not from how it sounds.
- `description`: How to spell true homophones — words that sound exactly alike but have different spellings and meanings, such as to/too/two and there/their/they're — by choosing the spelling that matches the sentence's meaning.
- FAQ question titles: "Are homophones the same as commonly confused words?"; "Are to/too/two and there/their/they're the only homophones my child needs to know?"; "My child spelled the word correctly, so why was it still marked wrong?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Word | Job in the sentence | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *to* | shows direction or connects to a verb | "Walk **to** the door." |
| *too* | means "also" or "more than enough" | "I want to come, **too**." |
| *two* | the number 2 | "I have **two** dogs." |


_Table 2:_

| Word | Job in the sentence | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *there* | tells where something is | "Put the book **there**." |
| *their* | shows something belongs to them | "That is **their** house." |
| *they're* | short for "they are" | "**They're** coming over." |


#### Commonly Confused Words
- Stable id: `commonly-confused-words`
- Canonical URL: `/skills/commonly-confused-words`
- Skill family: Homophones and Commonly Confused Words
- Family order: 12
- Skill order within family: 2
- Content role: `skill`
- Source file: `src/content/spelling-lists/grade-level/commonly-confused-words.md`
- Primary practice words, in exact stored order: `affect, effect, principal, principle, advice, advise, than, then`
- Word count: 8
- Grade curriculum placements: Grade 4: `grade-4-commonly-confused-words`; Grade 5: `grade-5-commonly-confused-words`
- Linked Grade Unit ids: `grade-4-commonly-confused-words`, `grade-5-commonly-confused-words`
- Prerequisite Skill ids: none
- Related Skill ids: `homophones`
- `shortAnswer`: Commonly confused words are pairs that get mixed up in writing for reasons beyond sound — similar spelling, similar meaning, or similar usage — such as affect/effect, principal/principle, advice/advise, and than/then. Some of these pairs sound alike and some don't; the way to choose the right one is always to check its meaning or its job in the sentence.
- `description`: How to tell apart commonly confused word pairs — such as affect/effect, principal/principle, advice/advise, and than/then — by meaning, part of speech, and context, since these pairs may or may not sound alike.
- FAQ question titles: "Are commonly confused words always homophones?"; "Is \"the principal is your pal\" a fair way to remember principal vs. principle, or is that a sound-based trick to avoid?"; "What should we do about a confusing pair that isn't on this list?"

**Additional distinct examples/comparison tables (verbatim from the content body; kept separate from primary practice words):**


_Table 1:_

| Word | Part of speech | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *affect* | verb — to influence something | "Rain can **affect** the game." |
| *effect* | noun — a result | "The rain had no **effect** on the score." |


_Table 2:_

| Word | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *principal* | a person in charge (or "main, most important") | "The **principal** greeted the class." |
| *principle* | a rule or belief | "Honesty is an important **principle**." |


_Table 3:_

| Word | Part of speech | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *advice* | noun — a suggestion | "She gave good **advice**." |
| *advise* | verb — to give a suggestion | "She will **advise** the team." |


_Table 4:_

| Word | Job in the sentence | Example |
|---|---|---|
| *than* | compares two things | "This book is longer **than** that one." |
| *then* | tells when something happens | "We ate lunch, **then** went outside." |

## Section 7 — Spelling-concept outline

_This extraction-only outline uses the live Core page titles and declared Skill links, then names the High-Frequency and Additional Practice sets._

### Kindergarten
- **Core Spelling:** Kindergarten First Words; Kindergarten Short A Words; Kindergarten Short I Words; Kindergarten Short O Words; Kindergarten Short U Words; Kindergarten Short E Words; Mixed Vowel CVC Review; Consonant Digraphs.
- **High-Frequency Words:** Kindergarten Common Words 1; Kindergarten Common Words 2; Kindergarten Common Words 3; Kindergarten Common Words 4.
- **Additional Practice:** Kindergarten Animal Words; Kindergarten Body Words; Kindergarten Number Words; Kindergarten Color Words; Kindergarten Family Words.

### Grade 1
- **Core Spelling:** CVC Short Vowel Review & The C/K Rule; The Floss Rule; Consonant Digraphs & Final -ck; Beginning Consonant Blends; Ending Consonant Blends; Long Vowels with Silent e (VCe); Short Words with Long Vowels: Open Syllables & Final Y; Long A & Long O Vowel Teams; Inflectional Endings: -s and -es; Inflectional Endings: -ed and -ing; R-Controlled Vowels: ar / or; Short Vowel Ending Rules: -tch and -dge.
- **High-Frequency Words:** Grade 1 Common Words 1; Grade 1 Common Words 2; Grade 1 Common Words 3; Grade 1 Common Words 4; Grade 1 Common Words 5; Grade 1 Common Words 6.
- **Additional Practice:** Grade 1 Weather Words; Grade 1 Clothing Words; Grade 1 Shape Words; Grade 1 Number Words 11–20; Grade 1 Days of the Week.

### Grade 2
- **Core Spelling:** OI and OY Words; OU and OW Words; Two Sounds of oo; Vowel Patterns: au and aw; Soft C and Soft G; Two-Syllable Words; Words Ending in -le; 2nd Grade Silent Letter Words; 2nd Grade Compound Words; 2nd Grade Contractions.
- **High-Frequency Words:** Grade 2 Common Words 1; Grade 2 Common Words 2; Grade 2 Common Words 3; Grade 2 Common Words 4; Grade 2 Common Words 5; Grade 2 Common Words 6.
- **Additional Practice:** Grade 2 Transportation Words; Grade 2 Money Words; Grade 2 Number Words 20–100; Grade 2 Community Helpers; Grade 2 Months of the Year.

### Grade 3
- **Core Spelling:** 3rd Grade Prefix Words; 3rd Grade Suffix Words; 3rd Grade Spelling Rule: Dropping Silent E; 3rd Grade Possessive Words; 3rd Grade Multisyllabic Words; 3rd Grade Homophones and Commonly Confused Words; 3rd Grade Root Word Families.
- **High-Frequency Words:** Grade 3 Common Words 1; Grade 3 Common Words 2; Grade 3 Common Words 3; Grade 3 Common Words 4; Grade 3 Common Words 5.
- **Additional Practice:** 3rd Grade Map & Globe Words; 3rd Grade Life Cycle Words; 3rd Grade Time Words; 3rd Grade Multiplication & Division Words.

### Grade 4
- **Core Spelling:** 4th Grade Multisyllabic Academic Words; 4th Grade Advanced Prefix Words; 4th Grade Advanced Suffix Words; 4th Grade Latin Root Words; 4th Grade Commonly Confused Words; 4th Grade Derived Words and Word Meaning.
- **High-Frequency Words:** Grade 4 Common Words 1; Grade 4 Common Words 2; Grade 4 Common Words 3; Grade 4 Common Words 4.
- **Additional Practice:** 4th Grade Measurement Words; 4th Grade Solar System Words; 4th Grade Career & Occupation Words; 4th Grade Geometry Words.

### Grade 5
- **Core Spelling:** 5th Grade Multisyllabic Academic Words; 5th Grade Prefix & Suffix Words; 5th Grade Greek & Latin Word Parts; 5th Grade Commonly Confused Words; 5th Grade Spelling Changes in Related Words.
- **High-Frequency Words:** Grade 5 Common Words 1; Grade 5 Common Words 2; Grade 5 Common Words 3; Grade 5 Common Words 4.
- **Additional Practice:** 5th Grade Money Management Words; 5th Grade Ecosystem & Environment Words; 5th Grade Fraction & Decimal Words; 5th Grade Civics and Government Words.

## Section 8 — Data-quality findings


No repository-grounded structural inconsistencies were found by the extraction checks: all manifest ids resolve, all canonical entries are published and contain words, ids and canonical paths are unique, Grade Hub membership/order matches the Grade manifest, all declared Grade Unit Skill ids resolve, and all Skills belong to a public family. Hub-card display copy was not treated as a title inconsistency because cards intentionally carry their own title field.
